2-Representations of Lie 2-groups and 2-Vector Bundles
Zhen Huan

Abstract
Murray, Roberts and Wockel showed that there is no strict model of the string 2-group using the free loop group. Instead, they construct the next best thing, a coherent model for the string 2-group using the free loop group, with explicit formulas for all structure. Based on their expectations, we build a category of 2-representations for coherent Lie 2-groups and some concrete examples. We also discuss the relation between this category of 2-representations and the category of representations. In addition, we construct a model of equivariant 2-vector bundles. At the end, we discuss the adjoint action on the string 2-representations.



1
Introduction

In this article, we explore the concept of 2-representation of a Lie 2-group on a more general type of 2-vector spaces. Since we wish our 2-representations to have fusion product eventually, we took some inspiration in
[
DR18
]
, and consider the strict 2-category 2
Vect
of k-prelinear categories. The objects are
k
-prelinear categories
, namely categories enriched in
k
-vector spaces. More precisely, a
k
-prelinear category is a category whose morphism sets are
k
-vector spaces such that compositions are
k
-bilinear. Here
k
is a field, which can be taken to be
\mathbb{C}
or
\mathbb{R}
from the angle of differential geometry especially. The 1-morphisms of
2\textsf{Vect}

are
k
-linear functors and 2-morphisms are natural transformations between
k-
linear functors. Notice that direct sum of vector spaces is a biproduct, a category enriched in
k
-vector spaces is an additive category.

There are other two types of 2-vector spaces, that people might be familiar with. One is the 2-vector space via categorification
[
BDR04
]
by Baez, Dundas and Rognes, and the other is Kapranov-Voevodsky’s 2-vector space
[
Kap99
]
. Philosophically
[
2ve13
]
our 2-vector spaces may be viewed as a sort of unification of these two.

Technically, to adapt to the differential geometric setting, we do not require our object to be finite (i.e. to have finite simple objects) because of the following reason: to make our construction work for Lie 2-groups, and include some basic examples, we wish to include the representation category of a Lie group, which is not finite in general. We also do not require our objects to be semisimple, however, we do prove in the article when will certain desired category be semisimple. We choose our setting like this to make it on one hand general enough to contain important examples and on the other hand conditioned enough to expect nice properties. We also wish our choice can be flexible enough, that we may adjust to obtain desirable results in the future plan.

The representation of a groupoid can be interpreted as a functor from it to the category of vector spaces. Motivated by the categorical interpretation of group representations, we define the category of 2-representations of a 2-group
\mathcal{G}
to be the bicategory whose objects are the functors from the delooping
B\mathcal{G}
to the category of 2-vector spaces, and whose 1-morphisms are transformations between them and whose 2-morphisms are modifications. As indicated in
[
MRW17
]
, the model of string 2-group which admits an action of the circle group on the loops cannot be provided by a strict 2-group. The next best model is given by a coherent Lie 2-group. This observation makes the 2-representations of a coherent 2-group more interesting to explore than strict 2-representations whereas the examples of coherent 2-representation can be much more complicated. We give the concrete and explicit construction of some examples afterwards. Our primary example of
\mathcal{G}
-representation sends the single object of
B\mathcal{G}
to the representation category of the underlying groupoid of
\mathcal{G}
. Other than those examples for coherent 2-representation, we also give the construction of 2-representation of cross modules to make this part complete.

Motivated by the construction of string principal bundles in
[
SXZ17
]
, we can build a model of 2-vector bundles.
Given a manifold
X
, we can define a bicategory
2\textsf{Vect}(X)
of categorical vector bundles whose fibers are
k-
linear categories, with gauge transformations as 1-morphisms and families of natural transformations as 2-morphisms. This gives a presheaf of bicategories on the site of smooth manifolds. Just as (1-)vector bundles form a
1-
stack, 2-vector bundles are expected to form a 2-stack. We obtain the 2-stack from the presheaf mentioned above by applying the plus construction of Nikolaus and Schweigert
[
NS11
]
. In addition, given a 2-vector bundle and a
\mathcal{G}
-representation, we can define a
\mathcal{G}
-principal bundle, which is a higher analogue of principal bundles.

In addition, as implied in
[
NS11
]
, the plus construction can be generalized to presheaves of bicategories on Lie groupoids. We show explicitly in this article that this is true. We apply it in the construction of equivariant 2-vector bundles. Recall a
G
-vector bundle over a
G
-space is a vector bundle
p:E\rightarrow X
with the projection
p
a
G
-map and the
G
-action on the fibres linear. The fibre of a 2-vector bundle is a 2-vector space, i.e. a
k
-linear category. To give a reasonable interpretation of equivariance, we define the action on a topological category by a topological groupoid based on the examples of the groupoid action on another groupoid in
[
MM03
, Section 5.3, page 126]
. Therefore, we can define an
\mathbb{G}
-equivariant 2-vector bundle with
\mathbb{G}
a groupoid. Locally, a
G
-equivariant (1-)vector bundle is a product vector bundle which may not still be
G
-equivariant but
H
-equivariant for some subgroup
H
of
G
.
In view of this, locally, an equivariant vector bundle should be the product of the base groupoid and a
k
-linear category, both of which have an action by a groupoid
\mathbb{G}
on it.
Then, applying the plus construction for presheaves on Lie groupoids, we build the 2-stack of equivariant 2-vector bundles.

One of our principal goals studying 2-representation and 2-vector bundles is explore string 2-group and its relation with physics. After sketching the construction of Murray, Roberts and Wockel’s coherent model of string 2-group,
we apply the general constructions to the model of string 2-group
[
MRW17
]
, define the string 2-representations and string 2-vector bundles.
We give some examples of string 2-representations. As indicated in
[
MRW17
, Section 4]
, for a good bicategory of coherent 2-representations, there should be a functor from the category of positive energy representations of the loop group to the category of 2-representations of string 2-group which preserves the fusion product. We haven’t found a good way to define the fusion product on the category of string 2-representations. But we do explicitly construct a functor from the category of 1-representation to that of 2-representation of
\mathcal{G}
if the underlying groupoid of
\mathcal{G}
is an action groupoid. When we take
\mathcal{G}
to be the string 2-group, we get a functor described above.

Another expectation in
[
MRW17
, Section 4]
is that
the category of positive energy representations
of
\widehat{LG}
could be the ”fixed points” under the adjoint action of
G
onto the category of Hilbert vector bundles
on
S(QG)
. To explore the exact conclusion, we define the adjoint action by
G
on the category of the string 2-representations.

2
Lie 2-groups and 2-representations

2.1
Basic concepts

Lie 2-group is a well established concept.

Definition 2.1
.

[
WZ05
, Definition.2.8]
Let
C
be a bicategory with finite products. A group object in
C
(or
C-
group, for brevity) consists of the following data:

•

an object
G
in
C

•

a list of 1-morphisms

–

m:G\times G\longrightarrow G
(the multiplication)

–

u:\ast\longrightarrow G
(the unit)

such that

(pr_{1},m):G\times G\longrightarrow G\times G

(2.1)

is an equivalence in
C
.

•

a list of invertible 2-morphisms

–

a:m\circ(m\times 1)\Rightarrow m\circ(1\times m)
(the associator)

–

l:m\circ(u\times 1)\Rightarrow 1
(the left unit constraint)

–

r:m\circ(1\times u)\Rightarrow 1
(the right unit constraint)

subject to the requirement that those coherence conditions
[
BL04
, Definition 2.8, p. 37]
, that is, the pentagon identity for associator, the triangle identity for the left and right unit laws. Here
1
is the identity morphism.

If
C=\mathsf{Mfd}
is the category of smooth manifold, then such a group object in
\mathsf{Mfd}
is a Lie 2-group.

We will also use
g\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}f
to denote their multiplication
m(g,f)
and use
I
to denote the image of the unit
u
in
G
.

Remark 2.2
.

As for the coherence conditions in Definition
2.1
, as indicated in
[
BL04
, Definition 2.8, p. 37]
, the associator
a
satisfies a pentagon diagram. In addition, we should have three diagrams for unit laws, as shown below:

\textstyle{(1\times u\times 1)\circ(m\times 1)\circ m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(1\times u\times 1)\circ a}
\scriptstyle{(r\times 1)\circ m}
\textstyle{(1\times u\times 1)\circ(1\times m)\circ m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(1\times 1)\circ m}
\textstyle{m}

(2.2)

\textstyle{(1\times 1\times u)\circ(m\times 1)\circ m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(1\times 1\times u)\circ a}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{(1\times 1\times u)\circ(1\times m)\circ m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(1\times r)\circ m}
\textstyle{m}

(2.3)

\textstyle{(u\times 1\times 1)\circ(m\times 1)\circ m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(u\times 1\times 1)\circ a}
\scriptstyle{(l\times 1)\circ m}
\textstyle{(u\times 1\times 1)\circ(1\times m)\circ m\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{l\circ m}
\textstyle{m}

(2.4)

However, as defined in the definition of coherent 2-group
[
BL04
, Definition 2.8, p. 37]
, the diagram (
2.2
) is sufficient. The reason is, from the coherent conditions in the definition, the maps
r:u\times u\longrightarrow u
and
l:u\times u\longrightarrow u
are the same map. Then similar to the proof of
[
Mac71
, Exercies 1, VII.1]
, we can show that the pentagon diagram of the associator
a
and the diagram (
2.2
) implies that (
2.3
) and (
2.4
) are both commutative (see the next lemma).

Lemma 2.3
.

The pentagon diagram of the associator
a
and (
2.2
) implies (
2.3
) and (
2.4
).

Proof.

Let
g
and
f
be two objects in
G
.

From the pentagon diagram for
((gf)I)I
and the diagram (
2.2
), we have the commutative diagrams

\textstyle{(gf)I}
\textstyle{((gf)I)I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{(gf)I}}
\scriptstyle{a_{gf,I,I}}
\scriptstyle{a_{g,f,I}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}1_{I}}
\textstyle{(g(fI))I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{g,fI,I}}
\textstyle{(gf)(II)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{gf}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}l_{I}}
\scriptstyle{a_{g,f,II}}
\textstyle{g((fI)I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{g}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}a_{f,I,I}}
\scriptstyle{1_{g}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}r_{fI}}
\textstyle{g(f(II))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{g}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}(1_{f}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}l_{I})}
\textstyle{g(fI)}

(2.5)

Moreover, by naturality, we have

\textstyle{(gf)(II)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\scriptstyle{1_{gf}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}r_{I}}
\textstyle{g(f(II))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{g}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}(1_{f}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}r_{I})}
\textstyle{(gf)I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{g(fI)}

(2.6)

Thus, (
2.5
) and (
2.6
) give us the commutative pentagon diagram below.

\textstyle{((gf)I)I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{g,f,I}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}1_{I}}
\scriptstyle{r_{(gf)I}}
\textstyle{(g(fI))I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{g,fI,I}}
\scriptstyle{r_{g(fI)}}
\textstyle{g((fI)I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{g}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}r_{fI}}
\textstyle{(gf)I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{g,f,I}}
\textstyle{g(fI)}

(2.7)

By naturality, the left square diagram commutes. Thus, the triangle diagram on the right commutes.

Finally, we apply the naturality of
r
we have the commutative diagram

\textstyle{(gf)I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{gf}}
\scriptstyle{a_{g,f,I}}
\textstyle{g(fI)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1_{g}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}r_{f}}
\textstyle{gf}

(2.8)

∎

2.2
2-Representations

Recall a representation of a group
G
is a group morphism from
G\to\Aut(V)
for a vector space
V
. This is a functor from
BG
, i.e. the group
G
viewed as a category with a single object
*
, to the category
Vect
of vector spaces. The single object
pt
maps to an object
V
in
Vect
, and each element
g
in
G
maps to an automorphism of
V
. Thus a 2-functor (which is called a morphism, in the terminology of bicategories
[
B6́7
]
) from
B\mathcal{G}
, a 2-group
\mathcal{G}
viewed as a 2-category with a single object
*
, to another 2-category
\mathcal{D}
is called a
2-representation
of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathcal{D}
, or we simply say that
\mathcal{G}

represents
on
\mathcal{D}
. These 2-representations form again a 2-category, that is

2\textsf{Rep}(B\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D})=\Hom(B\mathcal{G},\mathcal{D}),

(2.9)

is the 2-category of morphisms, transformations, and modifications.

We choose
\mathcal{D}
to be the strict 2-category of 2
Vect
, whose objects are
k
-prelinear categories
, namely categories enriched in
k
-vector spaces. More precisely, a
k
-prelinear category is a category whose morphism sets are
k
-vector spaces such that compositions are
k
-bilinear. Here
k
is a field (which is usually considered to be
\mathbb{C}
or
\mathbb{R}
–for diff. geom, we need to see where it doesn’t pass for
\mathbb{R}
). The 1-morphisms of
2\textsf{Vect}

are
k
-linear functors and 2-morphisms are natural transformations between
k-
linear functors. Notice that direct sum of vector spaces is a biproduct, a category enriched in
k
-vector spaces is an additive category.

Example 2.4

(Category of groupoid representations)
.

Notice that for a discrete groupoid
\Gamma=(\Gamma_{1}\Rightarrow\Gamma_{0})
, we have an associated category
\textsf{Rep}^{\Gamma}
–the category with objects functors
\Gamma\to\textsf{Vect}
, and 1-morphisms, natural transformations between functors. This is the prototype of 2-vector space
1
1

1

It is denoted by
\textsf{Vect}^{\Gamma}
therein, but to differentiate with
\Gamma
graded vector spaces, when
\Gamma
is a group, we use the notion
\textsf{Rep}^{\Gamma}
and later we see that it is really the category of representation of
\Gamma
.
proposed in
[
MRW17
]
. This is not a set anymore, but rather a category: an object in
\textsf{Rep}^{\Gamma}
is a functor
\rho
,

\rho:x\mapsto V_{x},\quad(a:x\to y)\mapsto(V_{x}\xrightarrow{\rho(a)}V_{y}),

where
\rho(a)
is a linear map, and
\rho(ab)=\rho(a)\rho(b)
. So it’s a representation of
\Gamma
on
Vect
. If
\Gamma
is further more a Lie groupoid, this category should be replaced by the representation category (let us use the same notation)
\textsf{Rep}^{\Gamma}
of the Lie groupoid
\Gamma
. Let us recall that a representation of Lie groupoid
\Gamma
, that is a vector bundle
V
over
\Gamma_{0}
, together with a Lie groupoid morphism
\Gamma\xrightarrow{\rho}\Gl(V)
, the generalised linear Lie groupoid of
V
(see
[
Mac00
]
); a morphism of
\textsf{Rep}^{\Gamma}
is a
\Gamma
-equivariant morphism. The differential geometry aspect of Lie groupoid makes this category not abelian.

Example 2.5

(Morphism in 2
\textsf{Rep}\mathcal{G}
)
.

Let us write down explicit data for a morphism
T
in 2
\textsf{Rep}\mathcal{G}
, based on the definition of the transformation between morphisms between bicategories
[
Lei98
, Section 1.2]
.

\mathcal{G}
-2-representations are morphisms from
B\mathcal{G}
to
2\textsf{Vect}
. Let
(D_{1},F_{1},\eta_{1},\phi_{1},\psi_{1})
and
(D_{2},F_{2},\eta_{2},\phi_{2},\psi_{2})
denote such two objects, where, for
i=1,2
,

•

each
D_{i}
is an object in
2\textsf{Vect}
, i.e. a
k
-prelinear category,

•

each

F_{i}:G_{0}\rightarrow Aut(D_{i}),\quad g_{0}\mapsto F_{i,g_{0}}

is a functor,

•

each

\displaystyle\eta_{i}:G_{1}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\{\mbox{Natural Transformations}\}

\displaystyle g_{1}=(g_{0}\xrightarrow{h}g_{0}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mapsto(F_{i,g_{0}}\xrightarrow{\eta_{i,g_{1}}}F_{i,g_{0}^{\prime}}).

•

\phi_{i}:m^{\prime}_{i}\circ((F_{i},\eta_{i})\times(F_{i},\eta_{i}))\Rightarrow(F_{i},\eta_{i})\circ m

and

\psi_{i}:u^{\prime}_{i}\Rightarrow(F_{i},\eta_{i})\circ u_{i}

are the natural transformations.

(2.10)

A transformation
T:(D_{1},F_{1},\eta_{1},\phi_{1},\psi_{1})\longrightarrow(D_{2},F_{2},\eta_{2},\phi_{2},\psi_{2})
is defined by the following data and axioms.

•

Data

1.

A morphism
T(\ast):D_{1}\longrightarrow D_{2}
in
2\textsf{Vect}
, i.e. a functor between the
k-
prelinear categories.

2.

a natural transformation

\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces G_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nearrow_{T}}
\scriptstyle{F_{1}}
\scriptstyle{F_{2}}
\textstyle{\textsf{Auto}(D_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\ast)_{*}}
\textstyle{\textsf{Auto}(D_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\ast)^{*}}
\textstyle{\Fun(D_{1},D_{2})}

(2.11)

Above, the notation
T(\ast)_{*}
denotes the functor

\textsf{Auto}(D_{1})\rightarrow\Fun(D_{1},D_{2}),\quad f\mapsto T(\ast)\circ f

and the notation
T(\ast)^{*}
denotes the functor

\textsf{Auto}(D_{2})\rightarrow\Fun(D_{1},D_{2}),\quad f\mapsto f\circ T(\ast)

Thus, for any
g_{0}\in G_{0}
, we have the natural transformation

T_{g_{0}}:F_{2,g_{0}}\circ T(\ast)\Rightarrow T(\ast)\circ F_{1,g_{0}}.

•

Axioms

The diagrams below commute.

1.

\textstyle{(F_{2,g_{0}}\circ F_{2,h_{0}})\circ T(\ast)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{2,(g_{0},h_{0})}\mathbin{\circ_{\textup{h}}}id_{T(\ast)}}
\textstyle{F_{2,g_{0}\circ h_{0}}\circ T(\ast)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{g_{0}\circ h_{0}}}
\textstyle{F_{2,g_{0}}\circ(F_{2,h_{0}}\circ T(\ast))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{id_{F_{2,g_{0}}}\mathbin{\circ_{\textup{h}}}T_{h_{0}}}
\textstyle{F_{2,g_{0}}\circ(T(\ast)\circ F_{1,h_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(F_{2,g_{0}}\circ T(\ast))\circ F_{1,h_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{g_{0}}\mathbin{\circ_{\textup{h}}}Id_{F_{1,h_{0}}}}
\textstyle{(T(\ast)\circ F_{1,g_{0}})\circ F_{1,h_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T(\ast)\circ(F_{1,g_{0}}\circ F_{1,h_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{Id_{T(\ast)}\mathbin{\circ_{\textup{h}}}\phi_{1,(g_{0},h_{0})}}
\textstyle{T(\ast)\circ F_{1,g_{0}\circ h_{0}}}

(2.12)

2.

\textstyle{I^{\prime}_{2}\circ T(\ast)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{l}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{2}\mathbin{\circ_{\textup{h}}}Id_{T(\ast)}}
\textstyle{T(\ast)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r^{-1}}
\textstyle{T(\ast)\circ I^{\prime}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1\mathbin{\circ_{\textup{h}}}\phi_{1}}
\textstyle{F_{2}I\circ T(\ast)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T_{I}}
\textstyle{T(\ast)\circ F_{1}I}

(2.13)

Lemma 2.6
.

For a Lie groupoid
\Gamma
,
\textsf{Rep}^{\Gamma}
is a category enriched in
k
-vector spaces. However, it is not an abelian category

Proof.

We need to verify that
\textsf{Rep}^{\Gamma}
is a category whose morphism sets are
k
-vector spaces and compositions are
k
-bilinear. A morphism
\rho\xrightarrow{f}\rho^{\prime}
in
\textsf{Rep}^{\Gamma}
, is a
\Gamma
-equivariant morphism linear fiberwise, i.e.
f_{x}\in\Hom(V_{x},V^{\prime}_{x})
for every point
x\in\Gamma_{0}
. Thus the linear structure of
\Hom(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
comes from the linear structure fiberwise.

It is not hard to verify that the composition is bilinear. We show that for
f\in\Hom(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
and
f^{\prime}\in\Hom(\rho^{\prime},\rho^{\prime\prime})
, for any
\alpha\in k
,
x\in\Gamma_{0}
, any vector
v
,
f_{x}\circ(\alpha f^{\prime}_{x}(v))=\alpha(f_{x}\circ f^{\prime}_{x})(v)
and
(\alpha f_{x})\circ f^{\prime}_{x}(v)=\alpha(f_{x}\circ f^{\prime}_{x}(v)).

Thus, the composition is bilinear.

This category is unfortunately not abelian, because the kernel
\ker(f)
for a morphism
f
without constant rank might not be a vector bundle again, therefore, not a representation of
\Gamma
. Thus it is not a
k
-linear category, which is defined to be an abelian category enriched in
k
-vector spaces.
∎

2.3
Examples of 2-representations

Lemma 2.7
.

A 2-group
\mathcal{G}=G_{1}\Rightarrow G_{0}
represents on 2
Vect
in the following way

•

Object level: the only object
pt
in
B\mathcal{G}
, maps to
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}
. Here
\mathcal{G}
in
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}
also denotes the underlying Lie groupoid of the 2-group by abusing of notation.

•

1-Morphism level:

G_{0}\to\Fun(\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}},\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}),\quad g\mapsto F_{g},

with

\begin{split}&F_{g}(\rho):x\mapsto\rho(x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g)(=V_{x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g}),\quad(x\xrightarrow{a}y)\mapsto(V_{x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g}\xrightarrow{\rho(a\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}1_{g})}V_{y\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g})\\
&F_{g}(\rho\xrightarrow{f}\rho^{\prime})=(F_{g}(\rho)\xrightarrow{F_{g}(f)}F_{g}(\rho^{\prime})),\quad\text{$F_{g}(f)$ is a natural transformation with $F_{g}(f)_{x}=f_{x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g}$,}\end{split}

for all
x,y\in G_{0}
,
(x\xrightarrow{a}y)\in G_{1}
, and
1
-morphism
f
in
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}
, which is in turn a natural transformation
\rho\Rightarrow\rho^{\prime}
. Here
\Fun(-,-)
denotes the set of functors.

•

2-Morphism level: given
(g_{0}\xrightarrow{g_{1}}g^{\prime}_{0})\in G_{1}

G_{1}\to\{\text{natural transformations }\},\quad g_{1}\mapsto\eta_{g_{1}},

\eta_{g_{1}}(\rho)_{x}:F_{g_{0}}(\rho)(x)\to F_{g^{\prime}_{0}}(\rho)(x),\quad v\mapsto\rho(1_{x}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{1})(v),\quad\forall v\in F_{g_{0}}(\rho)(x),

(2.14)

where
1_{x}
denotes the identity arrow in
G_{1}
at
x\in G_{0}
.

Proof.

On the level of 1-morphisms, we need to prove that
F_{g}(\rho)
is indeed a functor,
F_{g}(f)
is indeed a natural transformation, and
F_{g}
is
k
-linear, that is,
F_{g}(f+f^{\prime})=F_{g}(f)+F_{g}(f^{\prime})
and
F_{g}(\lambda f)=\lambda F_{g}(f)
for
\lambda\in k
. The fact that
F_{g}(\rho)
is a functor is obvious, and the
k
-linearity of
F_{g}
follows from that of
\Hom
-space of
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}
.

The fact that
F_{g}(f)
is indeed a natural transformation follows from the fact that
f
is a natural transformation. Indeed, given
x\xrightarrow{b}y\in\mathcal{G}
, the diagram

\textstyle{F_{g}(\rho)(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{g}(f)_{x}}
\scriptstyle{F_{g}(\rho)(b)}
\textstyle{F_{g}(\rho^{\prime})(x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{g}(\rho^{\prime})(b)}
\textstyle{F_{g}(\rho)(y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{g}(f)_{y}}
\textstyle{F_{g}(\rho^{\prime})(y)}

(2.15)

i.e., the diagram

\textstyle{\rho(x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f(x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g)}
\scriptstyle{\rho(b\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}1_{g})}
\textstyle{\rho^{\prime}(x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\prime}(b\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}1_{g})}
\textstyle{\rho(y\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f(y\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g)}
\textstyle{\rho^{\prime}(y\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g)}

(2.16)

commutes because
f
is a natural transformation.

Now on the level of 2-morphisms, we need to check that, given
(g_{0}\xrightarrow{g_{1}}g^{\prime}_{0})\in G_{1}
, what we defined
\eta_{g_{1}}(\rho):F_{g_{0}}(\rho)\Rightarrow F_{g^{\prime}_{0}}(\rho)
is indeed a natural transformation. We need to check the commutativity of the following diagram for all
x\xrightarrow{b}y\in G_{1}
,

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 40.3988pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-24.40788pt\raise 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 48.63472pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 48.63472pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F_{g_{0}^{\prime}}(\rho)(y)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

(2.17)

which is in turn just

\textstyle{V_{x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho(b\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}1_{g_{0}})}
\scriptstyle{\rho(1_{x}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{1})}
\textstyle{V_{x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g^{\prime}_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho(b\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}1_{g^{\prime}_{0}})}
\textstyle{V_{y\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho(1_{y}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{1})}
\textstyle{V_{y\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g^{\prime}_{0}}.}

(2.18)

This diagram follows from that fact that
\rho
is a functor and the following equation,

(b\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}1_{g^{\prime}_{0}})\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{v}}}(1_{x}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{1})=(b\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{v}}}1_{x})\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}(1_{g^{\prime}_{0}}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{v}}}g_{1})=b\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{1}=(1_{y}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{v}}}b)\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}(g_{1}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}1_{g_{0}})=(1_{y}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{1})\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{v}}}(b\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}1_{g_{0}}).

(2.19)

To show that the data in the above lemma gives us a morphism from
B\mathcal{G}
to 2
Vect
, we still need to verify the following (see
[
Lei98
, 1.1]
)

1)

(F,\eta):\mathcal{G}\to\textsf{Auto}(\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}})
from the category
\mathcal{G}
, (whose structure is the underlying Lie groupoid of the 2-group
\mathcal{G}
) to the category
\textsf{Auto}(\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}})
of autofunctors from
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}
to itself and the natural transformations between them.

2)

There exist natural transformations

\phi:m^{\prime}\circ((F,\eta)\times(F,\eta))\Rightarrow(F,\eta)\circ m

(2.20)

and

\psi:u^{\prime}\Rightarrow(F,\eta)\circ u.

(2.21)

(2.22)

Here
m^{\prime}(-,-)
denotes the composition of functors and horizontal contacnation of natural transformations in
\textsf{Auto}(\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}})
and
u^{\prime}:\ast\longrightarrow\textsf{Auto}(\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}})
is the unit in
\textsf{Auto}(\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}})
. Thus we write
m^{\prime}(F,F^{\prime})
also simply as
F\circ F^{\prime}
.

\phi
and
\psi
make the following diagrams commute. Here we use
I^{\prime}
to denote
u^{\prime}(\ast)
.

(2.25)

We now verify the three items one by one.

Verification of item 1):

To verify 1), we need to see that
(F,\eta)
preserves identities and compositions. That is, we need to verify that
\eta_{1_{g_{0}}}=\id
and
\eta_{g^{\prime}_{1}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{v}}}g_{1}}=\eta_{g^{\prime}_{1}}\circ\eta_{g_{1}}
. These follow from some direct calculation. The morphism
g_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{1_{g_{0}}}}g_{0}
is sent to
\eta_{1_{g_{0}}}
, and

\eta_{1_{g_{0}}}(\rho)_{x}:F_{g_{0}}(\rho)(x)\longrightarrow F_{g_{0}}(\rho)(x),\quad v\mapsto\rho(1_{x}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}1_{g_{0}})(v)=\rho(1_{x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{0}})(v)=v.

For
x\in G_{0}
,
g_{1},g^{\prime}_{1}\in G_{1}
, and
\rho
an object of
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}
,

\eta_{g^{\prime}_{1}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{v}}}g_{1}}(\rho)_{x}=\rho(1_{x}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}(g^{\prime}_{1}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{v}}}g_{1}))=\rho((1_{x}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g^{\prime}_{1})\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{v}}}(1_{x}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{1}))=\rho(1_{x}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g^{\prime}_{1})\circ\rho(1_{x}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{1})=(\eta_{g^{\prime}_{1}}\circ\eta_{g_{1}})(\rho)_{x}

Verification of item 2):
it is easy to see that

\displaystyle F_{g_{0}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}\hat{g}_{0}}(\rho)(x)

\displaystyle=\rho(x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}(g_{0}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}\hat{g}_{0}))

(2.26)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\rho(a^{-1}_{x,g_{0},\hat{g}_{0}})}\rho((x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{0})\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}\hat{g}_{0})\mbox{ where $a_{x,g_{0},\hat{g}_{0}}$ is the associator of }\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}

(2.27)

\displaystyle=F_{g_{0}}(F_{\hat{g}_{0}}(\rho))(x)=F_{g_{0}}\circ F_{\hat{g}_{0}}(\rho)(x).

(2.28)

This shows that there is a natural transformation (in fact isomorphism)
F_{g_{0}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}\hat{g}_{0}}\xrightarrow{\phi_{(g_{0},\hat{g}_{0})}}F_{g_{0}}\circ F_{\hat{g}_{0}}
between these two autofunctors, given by

(\phi_{(g_{0},\hat{g}_{0})})(\rho)=\rho(a^{-1}_{-,g_{0},\hat{g}_{0}})

(2.29)

at each object
\rho
in
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}
,

F_{g_{0}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}\hat{g}_{0}}(\rho)\xrightarrow{\rho(a^{-1}_{-,g_{0},\hat{g}_{0}})}(F_{g_{0}}\circ F_{\hat{g}_{0}})(\rho).

Now we verify that
\phi_{(g_{0},\hat{g}_{0})}
defined above is indeed a natural transformation. Let
\alpha:\rho\Rightarrow\rho^{\prime}
be a 1-morphism in
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}
. Then we need to show the diagram commutes

\textstyle{F_{g_{0}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}\hat{g}_{0}}(\rho)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{g_{0}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}\hat{g}_{0}}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{{\rho(a^{-1}_{-,g_{0},\hat{g}_{0}})}}
\textstyle{(F_{g_{0}}\circ F_{\hat{g}_{0}})(\rho)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(F_{g_{0}}\circ F_{\hat{g}_{0}})(\alpha)}
\textstyle{F_{g_{0}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}\hat{g}_{0}}(\rho^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\prime}(a^{-1}_{-,g_{0},\hat{g}_{0}})}
\textstyle{(F_{g_{0}}\circ F_{\hat{g}_{0}})(\rho^{\prime}).}

(2.30)

It suffices to show the diagram below commutes for any
x

\textstyle{\rho(x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}(g_{0}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}\hat{g}_{0}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha(x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}(g_{0}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}\hat{g}_{0}))}
\scriptstyle{{\rho(a^{-1}_{x,g_{0},\hat{g}_{0}})}}
\textstyle{\rho((x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{0})\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}\hat{g}_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha((x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{0})\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}\hat{g}_{0})}
\textstyle{\rho^{\prime}(x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}(g_{0}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}\hat{g}_{0}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\prime}(a^{-1}_{x,g_{0},\hat{g}_{0}})}
\textstyle{\rho^{\prime}((x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{0})\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}\hat{g}_{0}).}

(2.31)

which commutes because of the naturality of the associator.
Then
\phi:m^{\prime}\circ((F,\eta)\times(F,\eta))\Rightarrow(F,\eta)\circ m
, given by
\phi_{(g_{0},\hat{g}_{0})}
at each object
(g_{0},\hat{g}_{0})\in G_{0}\times G_{0}
is the natural transformation that we desire. The naturality of
\phi
,

(2.32)

follows from the naturality of
a
and the functoriality of
\rho
.

Now we construct the natural transformation
\psi
in (
2.22
) by

\psi_{\ast}:I^{\prime}=u^{\prime}(\ast)\to(F,\eta)(\ast)=F_{I},\quad\psi_{\ast}(\rho)(x)=\rho(r_{x})^{-1}.

(2.33)

The natruality of
r
and the functoriality of
\rho
implies that
\psi_{*}
is indeed a natural transformation between the two autofunctors
I^{\prime}
and
F_{I}
. Then
the naturality of
\psi
is trivial to be shown because there is only identity morphism in
\ast
.

Verification of item 3):

The 6-arrow diagram (
2.23
) when evaluated on an object
\rho
in
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}
becomes

\textstyle{(F_{g_{0}}\circ F_{h_{0}})\circ F_{f_{0}}(\rho)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{F_{(g_{0}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}h_{0})\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}f_{0}}(\rho)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{F_{g_{0}}\circ(F_{h_{0}}\circ F_{f_{0}})(\rho)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{F_{g_{0}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}(h_{0}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}f_{0})}(\rho)}

(2.34)

Notice that
\id_{F_{f_{0}}}(\rho)=\rho(\id_{f_{0}})
, and when we further evaluate on
x\in G_{0}
, the natural transformation
\rho(a_{-,g_{0},h_{0}})\mathbin{\circ_{\textup{h}}}\rho(\id_{f_{0}})
at

as a morphism
\rho(((x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{0})\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}h_{0})\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}f_{0})\to\rho((x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}(g_{0}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}h_{0}))\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}f_{0})
, is exactly
\rho(a_{x,g_{0},h_{0}}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}\id_{f_{0}})
. So the diagram follows from the pentagon condition of
a
and the functoriality of
\rho
.

In the diagram (
2.24
) the composition

\displaystyle(\eta_{l}\mathbin{\circ_{\textup{h}}}\phi^{-1}_{(I,g_{0})})(\rho)(x)

\displaystyle=\rho(\id_{x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}I}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}l_{g_{0}})\mathbin{\circ_{\textup{h}}}\rho(a_{x,I,g_{0}}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\rho(r_{x})\mathbin{\circ_{\textup{h}}}\id_{I^{\prime}}

Thus,
(\eta_{I}\mathbin{\circ_{\textup{h}}}\phi^{-1}_{I,g_{0}})(\rho)(x)\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}\psi(\rho)(x)
is identity.

By Lemma
2.3
, we also have the commutative diagram

\textstyle{(x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{0})\cdot I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{a_{x,g_{0},I}}
\scriptstyle{r_{x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{0}}}
\textstyle{x\cdot(g_{0}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}I)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{g_{0}}}
\textstyle{x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{0}}

(2.35)

Thus,
in the diagram (
2.25
) the composition

\displaystyle(\eta_{r}\mathbin{\circ_{\textup{h}}}\phi^{-1}_{g_{0},I})(\rho)(x)

\displaystyle=\rho(\id\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}r_{g_{0}})\mathbin{\circ_{\textup{h}}}\rho(a_{x,g_{0},I}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\rho((\id\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}r_{g_{0}})\mathbin{\circ_{\textup{h}}}a_{x,g_{0},I}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\rho(r_{x\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{0}})

Thus, the composition

Suppose there is a Lie 2-group
\mathcal{G}=(G_{1}\Rightarrow G_{0})
whose underlining Lie groupoid is an action groupoid, that is,
G_{0}=G
,
G_{1}=H\ltimes G
and
\mathsf{s}(h,g)=g
,
\mathsf{t}(h,g)=h\cdot g
,
(h_{1},g_{1})(h_{2},g_{2})=(h_{1}h_{2},g_{2})
, where
H
acts on
G
from the left with
\cdot
.
Let
\textsf{Rep}_{tr}^{\mathcal{G}}
denote the full sub-category of
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}
which consists of trivial vector bundles. Then a representation
\rho\in\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}_{tr}
reads for us a trivial vector bundle
V\times G
and
\rho(h,g)\in\End(V)
. Therefore, it is not hard to see that an
H
-representation
\rho_{0}
gives us an element
\rho\in\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}_{tr}
, by

\rho(h,g):=\rho_{0}(h).

In fact, we may further see that

\textsf{Rep}(H)\xrightarrow{i}\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}_{tr}
is a full subcategory.

Corollary 2.8
.

For a Lie 2-group
\mathcal{G}
, there is another 2-representation similar to what is given in Lemma
2.7
but restricted to the sub category
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}_{tr}
, that is, we replace
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}
by
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}_{tr}
everywhere. More precisely, there is a 2-representation of
\mathcal{G}
with

•

Object level: the only object
*
in the 2-category corresponding to a 2-group, maps to
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}_{tr}
.

•

1-Morphism level:

F:G_{0}\to\Fun(\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}_{tr},\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}_{tr}),\quad g\mapsto F_{g},

where

\begin{split}F_{g}(\rho):x\mapsto V,\quad(x\xrightarrow{a}y)\mapsto(V\xrightarrow{\rho(a\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}1_{g})}V)\\
F_{g}(\rho\xrightarrow{f}\rho^{\prime})=F_{g}(\rho)\xrightarrow{F_{g}(f)=f}F_{g}(\rho^{\prime})\end{split}

where
\rho\in\textsf{Rep}_{tr}^{\mathcal{G}}
comes with the trivial bundle
V\times G_{0}
, and
f
is a 1-morphism in
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}
, thus also in
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}_{tr}
.

•

2-Morphism level: given
(g_{0}\xrightarrow{g_{1}}g^{\prime}_{0})\in G_{1}

G_{1}\to\{\text{natural transformations }\},\quad g_{1}\mapsto\eta_{g_{1}},

\eta_{g_{1}}(\rho)_{x}:F_{g_{0}}(\rho)(x)\to F_{g^{\prime}_{0}}(\rho)(x),\quad v\mapsto\rho(1_{x}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}g_{1})(v),\quad\forall v\in F_{g_{0}}(\rho)(x).

(2.36)

Proof.

The 2-representation we describe above is what we have in Lemma
2.7
but restricting to
\textsf{Rep}_{tr}^{\mathcal{G}}
. Because the inclusion
\textsf{Rep}_{tr}^{\mathcal{G}}\subset\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}
is fully faithful, this gives us another 2-representation of
\mathcal{G}
.
∎

2.4
Relation between 1-representations and 2-representations

Given a representation
(V,\zeta)
of a Lie group
H
, we form a category
\{V\}/\zeta(H)
with just one object
V
. The morphisms form the set
\zeta(H)
with arrows from
V
to
V
given in the obvious way. Then given an action Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}=H\ltimes G\Rightarrow G
, we have a
k
-linear category,

C_{V}:=\Fun(\mathcal{G},\{V\}/\zeta(H)),

and clearly
C_{V}
is a subcategory of
\textsf{Rep}^{tr}_{\mathcal{G}}
. In a differential geometric viewpoint,
C_{V}
is the category of special trivial representations of
\mathcal{G}
with objects
(G\times V,\rho)
where
\rho(h,g)
is the arrow
(hg,\zeta(h)(v))\leftarrow(g,v)
.
Similarly, we have

Corollary 2.9
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a Lie 2-group with underline Lie groupoid given by an action groupoid
H\ltimes G\Rightarrow G
, and let
(V,\zeta)
be a representation of
H
. Then there is another 2-representation of
\mathcal{G}
similar to what is given in Lemma
2.7
and Cor.
2.8
but restricted to the sub category
C_{V}
, that is, we replace
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}
by
C_{V}
everywhere. We denote this 2-representation of
\mathcal{G}
by
(f_{V},\eta_{V})
.

With these results, we have the following theorem.

Theorem 2.10
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a Lie 2-group with underline Lie groupoid given by an action groupoid
H\ltimes G\Rightarrow G
, there is a functor

i:\textsf{Rep}H\longrightarrow 2\textsf{Rep}\mathcal{G}

defined as follows.

•

On the level of objects: for
(V,\zeta)\in\textsf{Rep}H
,
i(V,\zeta):=(f_{V},\eta_{V})
as in Cor.
2.9
.

•

On the level of morphisms:
for each morphism

(V_{0},\zeta_{0})\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{\omega}}(V_{1},\zeta_{1})

in
\textsf{Rep}H
, we define a morphism

i(\omega)

i(V_{0},\zeta_{0})\xrightarrow{(T(\ast),T_{\omega})}i(V_{1},\zeta_{1})

in 2
\textsf{Rep}\mathcal{G}
below.
For each
h\in H
, we have the commutative diagram

\textstyle{(V_{0},\zeta_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{0}(h)}
\textstyle{(V_{1},\zeta_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{1}(h)}
\textstyle{(V_{0},\zeta_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\textstyle{(V_{1},\zeta_{1}).}

For each
\omega
, we can define a functor

F_{\omega}:\{V_{0}\}/\zeta_{0}(H)\to\{V_{1}\}/\zeta_{1}(H)

by sending the object

V_{0}
to
V_{1}
and sending each morphism
\zeta_{0}(h)
to
\zeta_{1}(h)
.
Moreover, we can define a functor

F_{\omega*}:C_{V_{0}}\longrightarrow C_{V_{1}}

by sending a morphism

\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{\rho}\{V_{0}\}/\zeta_{0}(H)

to

\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{\rho}\{V_{0}\}/\zeta_{0}(H)\xrightarrow{F_{\omega}}\{V_{1}\}/\zeta_{1}(H).

Then we are ready to define the morphism
i(\omega)

i(V_{0},\zeta_{0})\xrightarrow{(T(\ast),T_{\omega})}i(V_{1},\zeta_{1})

in 2
\textsf{Rep}\mathcal{G}
in the way below.

1.

\displaystyle T(\ast):=F_{\omega*}:C_{V_{0}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow C_{V_{1}}

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{\rho}\{V_{0}\}/\zeta_{0}(H)\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{\rho}\{V_{0}\}/\zeta_{0}(H)\xrightarrow{F_{\omega}}\{V_{1}\}/\zeta_{1}(H)\big)

2.

The natural transformation
T_{\omega}

\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces G_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nearrow_{T_{\omega}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{V_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{V_{1}}}
\textstyle{\textsf{Auto}(C_{V_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\ast)_{*}}
\textstyle{\textsf{Auto}(C_{V_{1}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\ast)^{*}}
\textstyle{\Fun(C_{V_{0}},C_{V_{1}})}

is defined to be identity.

Proof.

The functor
i
defined in the theorem satisfies the axioms in Example
2.5
.
∎

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(G_{1}\Rightarrow G_{0})
be a Lie 2-group, then
\textsf{Rep}_{tr}^{\mathcal{G}}
is a category enriched in
k
-vector spaces and is a cocomplete
k
-linear category,
thus, a 2-vector space by the definition
[
Lur09
, Example 1.2.4]
.
If
Vect
is the category of finite dimensional vector spaces, then
\textsf{Rep}_{tr}^{\mathcal{G}}
is semisimple.

Proof.

As a subcategory of
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}
,
\textsf{Rep}_{tr}^{\mathcal{G}}
is a category enriched in
k
-vector spaces.
Notice that direct sum of vector spaces is a biproduct, a category enriched in
k
-vector spaces is an additive category.
Now we show that
\textsf{Rep}_{tr}^{\mathcal{G}}
is abelian. We define biproduct
\oplus
in
\textsf{Rep}_{tr}^{\mathcal{G}}
by

\rho_{1}\oplus\rho_{2}:\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow\GL(V_{1}\oplus V_{2}),\quad g\mapsto\rho_{1}(g)\oplus\rho_{2}(g),\quad\forall\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in\textsf{Rep}_{tr}^{\mathcal{G}},

(2.37)

where
V_{i}
is the trivial vector bundle over
G_{0}
on which
\mathcal{G}
represents by
\rho_{i}
.
Let
\underline{0}
denote the 0-dimensional vector bundle over
G_{0}
. The zero object in
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}_{tr}
is the morphism sending
\mathcal{G}
to
\GL(\underline{0})
.
Let
\rho_{i}
be an object in
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}_{tr}
whose image is a vector space
V_{i}
,
i=1,2
. Let
f:\rho_{1}\longrightarrow\rho_{2}
denote a morphism in
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}_{tr}
. Then it is an
H
-equivariant linear map from
V_{1}
to
V_{2}
. (And given any
H
-equivariant linear map from
V_{1}
to
V_{2}
, we can define a functor from
\rho_{1}
to
\rho_{2}
.)
The kernel of
f
is the functor
\ker f
from
\mathcal{G}
to the vector space
\ker f
, which is an
H-
invariant subspace of
V_{1}
. We have the evident monomorphism from
\ker f
to
\rho_{1}
.
And the cokernel of
f
is the functor from
\mathcal{G}
to the vector space
\coker f
, which is an
H-
invariant subspace of
V_{2}
. We have the evident epimorphism from
\coker f
to
\rho_{2}
.
Since the category of
H-
representations is an abelian category, for any
H-
equivariant linear map
h:V_{1}\longrightarrow V_{2}
, when it is a monomorphism, it is a kernel, and when it is a epimorphism, it is a cokernel.
Thus,
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}_{tr}
is also an abelian category.
The cocompleteness of
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}_{tr}
comes from the cocompleteness of the category of
H-
representations.
An object in
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}_{tr}
is simple if its image in
Vect
is simple.
If
Vect
is the category of finite-dimensional vector spaces, it is semisimple, i.e. each object in it is the direct sum of finitely many simple objects.
In this case, each object in
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}_{tr}
is the direct sum of finitely many simple objects. Thus,
\textsf{Rep}^{\mathcal{G}}_{tr}
is semisimple.
∎

2.5
2-Representations of cross modules

When the Lie 2-group is strict, there is another sort of 2-representation for them. Even though we will not use this sort of 2-representation to later for String Lie 2-groups, we will still demonstrate it here for some completeness. Let us first recall the definition of a strict Lie 2-group through the concept of a crossed module of Lie groups. For convenience, we use the version in
[
MRW17
]
with a right action because we will use their model on string 2-groups.

Definition 2.12
.

A crossed
module of Lie groups
(H\xrightarrow{\partial}G,\alpha)
consists of a Lie group morphism
\partial:H\to G
and a right action

\alpha
of
G
on
H
, namely a Lie group morphism
\alpha:G^{op}\to\Aut(H)
, such that

(Eq)

\partial(\alpha(g)h)=g^{-1}\partial(h)g
(Equivariance)

(Pf)

\alpha(\partial(h))h^{\prime}=h^{-1}h^{\prime}h
(Pfeiffer identity).

Such a crossed module of Lie groups gives us a strict Lie 2-group with the simplicial nerve

X_{0}=pt,\quad X_{1}=G,\quad X_{2}=G\times G\times H,\quad\dots X_{k}=G^{\times k}\times H^{(^{k}_{2})}.

Or we may see it as a group structure on the action Lie groupoid
H\ltimes G\Rightarrow G
, where
H
acts from left on
G
through

\partial
. More precisely, we have

\mathsf{t}(g_{1},h)=g_{2}=\partial(h)g_{1},\quad\mathsf{s}(g_{1},h)=g_{1},\quad\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bullet}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 47.50003pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\bullet}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 18.4724pt\raise 17.1875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{g_{2}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.50003pt\raise 12.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 3.3494pt\raise 2.94533pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise
