0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 26.19447pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.36725pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{h\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}h^{\prime}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

where
h\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}h^{\prime}=h\alpha(g^{-1}_{1})(h^{\prime})
. Indeed,

g_{2}g^{\prime}_{2}=\partial(h)g_{1}\partial(h^{\prime})g^{\prime}_{1}=\partial(h)g_{1}\partial(h^{\prime})g^{-1}_{1}g_{1}g^{\prime}_{1}=\partial(h\alpha^{\prime}(g_{1}^{-1})(h^{\prime}))g_{1}g^{\prime}_{1}.

Thus,
H\ltimes G\Rightarrow G
is a group object in the (strict) 2-category of Lie groupoid, Lie groupoid morphisms, and 2-morphisms. All the 2-morphisms in Definition
2.1
are identity. Therefore, it is a strict Lie 2-group.

From now on we do not distinguish strict Lie 2-groups and crossed modules.

Lemma 2.13
.

A crossed module
(H\xrightarrow{\partial}G,\alpha:G^{op}\longrightarrow Aut(H))
represents on
2\textsf{Vect}
in the following way

•

Object level: the only object
*
in the 2-category corresponding to a crossed module, which is a 2-group, maps to the category of
H
-representations, that is
*\mapsto\textsf{Rep}H
.

•

1-Morphism level:

G\to\Fun(\textsf{Rep}H,\textsf{Rep}H),\quad g\mapsto F_{g},

where

F_{g}\big((V,\rho)\big)=(V,\rho\circ\alpha(g)),\quad F_{g}\big((V,\rho)\xrightarrow{f}(V^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\big)=(V,\rho\circ\alpha(g))\xrightarrow{f}(V^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}\circ\alpha(g)).

Here
\rho:H\to\Aut(V)
is a representation of
H
and we denote such a representation by
(V,\rho)
.

•

2-Morphism level:

G\times H\to\{\text{natural transformations }\},\quad(g_{1},h)\mapsto\eta_{(g_{1},h)},

where

\eta_{(g_{1},h)}(V,\rho):\big(V,\rho\circ\alpha(g_{1})\big)\to\big(V,\rho\circ\alpha(g_{2})\big),\quad v\mapsto\rho(\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}))(v).

Proof.

We first check whether
F_{g}
is indeed a functor. For this we need to see whether
f:(V,\rho\circ\alpha(g))\to(V^{\prime},\rho^{\prime}\circ\alpha(g))
is still
H
-equivariant thus still a morphism between
H
-representations. This follows from a direct calculation,

f(h\cdot v)=f(\rho(\alpha(g)(h))v)=\rho^{\prime}(\alpha(g)(h))f(v)=h\cdot_{{}^{\prime}}f(v).

We now check whether
\eta_{(g_{1},h)}(V,\rho)
is equivariant:

\eta_{(g_{1},h)}(V,\rho)(\hat{h}\cdot v)=\rho(\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}))(\hat{h}\cdot v)=\rho(\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}))(\rho(\alpha(g_{1})(\hat{h}))(v))=\rho(\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1})\alpha(g_{1})(\hat{h}))(v),

and

\displaystyle\hat{h}\cdot_{{}^{\prime}}\eta_{(g_{1},h)}(V,\rho)(v)

\displaystyle=\rho(\alpha(g_{2})\hat{h})(\rho(\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}))v)

\displaystyle=\rho(\alpha(\partial(h)g_{1})(\hat{h}))(\rho(\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}))v)=\rho(\alpha(g_{1})\alpha(\partial(h))(\hat{h})\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}))(v)

\displaystyle=\rho(\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}\hat{h}h)\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}))(v)=\rho(\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}\hat{h}))(v)

\displaystyle=\rho(\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1})\alpha(g_{1})(\hat{h}))(v)=\eta(\hat{h}\cdot v)

where
g_{2}=\partial(h)g_{1}
. Thus,
\eta
is equivariant and thus it defines a morphism between
representations.

The following diagram

\textstyle{F_{g_{1}}\big((V,\rho)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{(g_{1},h)}(V,\rho)}
\scriptstyle{F_{g_{1}}(f)}
\textstyle{F_{g_{2}}\big((V,\rho)\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{g_{2}}(f)}
\textstyle{F_{g_{1}}\big((V^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{(g_{1},h)}(V^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})}
\textstyle{F_{g_{2}}\big((V^{\prime},\rho^{\prime})\big)}

(2.38)

commutes. In fact, this boils down to the fact that
f(\rho(\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}))v)=\rho^{\prime}(\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}))f(v)
, which is implied by the fact that
f
is a morphism of
representations. Thus
\eta
, that we construct, is a natural transformation.

Now to verify that these data gives us a morphism from
B\mathcal{G}
to
2\textsf{Vect}
, where
\mathcal{G}
is the strict Lie 2-group corresponds to our crossed module, we still need to verify three items as in Lemma
2.7
. But the strictness simplifies a lot our proof. Now we check these items one by one:

Verification of 1)
: We need to check whether

\eta_{(g_{2},h_{2})}\circ\eta_{(g_{1},h_{1})}=\eta_{(g_{2},h_{2})\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{v}}}(g_{1},h_{1})}=\eta_{(g_{1},h_{2}h_{1})}.

(2.39)

Consider the composition

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 23.07799pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-23.07799pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F_{g_{1}}(V,\rho)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.33185pt\raise 7.04443pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.20557pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta_{(g_{1},h_{1})}(V,\rho)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 47.07799pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 47.07799pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F_{g_{2}}(V,\rho)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 79.48782pt\raise 7.04443pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.20557pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta_{(g_{2},h_{2})}(V,\rho)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 117.23396pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 117.23396pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F_{g_{3}}(V,\rho)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

\displaystyle\eta_{(g_{2},h_{2})}(V,\rho)\circ\eta_{(g_{1},h_{1})}(V,\rho)(v)

(2.40)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta_{(g_{2},h_{2})}(V,\rho)(\rho(\alpha(g_{1})(h_{1}^{-1}))(v))=\rho(\alpha(g_{2})(h_{2}^{-1}))\rho(\alpha(g_{1})(h_{1}^{-1}))(v)

(2.41)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho(\alpha(\partial(h_{1})g_{1})(h_{2}^{-1})\alpha(g_{1})(h_{1}^{-1}))(v)=\rho(\alpha(g_{1})\alpha(\partial(h_{1}))(h_{2}^{-1})\alpha(g_{1})(h_{1}^{-1}))(v)

(2.42)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho(\alpha(g_{1})(h_{1}^{-1}h_{2}^{-1}h_{1}h_{1}^{-1}))(v)=\rho(\alpha(g_{1})((h_{2}h_{1})^{-1}))(v)

(2.43)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\eta_{(g_{1},h_{2}h_{1})}(V,\rho)(v)=\eta_{(g_{2},h_{2})\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{v}}}(g_{1},h_{1})}(V,\rho)(v)

(2.44)

Verification of 2) and 3)
: We see that
F_{g\hat{g}}=F_{g}\circ F_{\hat{g}}
,

F_{g\hat{g}}(V,\rho)=(V,\rho\circ\alpha(g\hat{g}))=(V,\rho\circ\alpha(\hat{g})\circ\alpha(g))=F_{g}(V,\rho\circ\alpha(\hat{g}))=F_{g}F_{\hat{g}}(V,\rho).

Therefore the natural transformation
\phi
in (
2.20
) is
\id
. It is then similar to show that
\psi
in (
2.21
) is also
\id
. Thus the coherence conditions in 3) automatically hold.
∎

Remark 2.14
.

In the above setting, we see that there is a horizontal composition of the natural transformation. Let’s consider the functors
F_{g_{1}}
,
F_{g_{1}^{\prime}}
,
F_{g_{2}}
,
F_{g_{2}^{\prime}}
:
\textsf{Rep}H\to\textsf{Rep}H
and the natural transformations

\eta_{(g_{1},h)}:F_{g_{1}}\Longrightarrow F_{g_{2}},\quad\eta_{(g_{1}^{\prime},h^{\prime})}:F_{g_{1}^{\prime}}\Longrightarrow F_{g_{2}^{\prime}}.

We will show the two maps

\textstyle{F_{g^{\prime}_{1}}\circ F_{g_{1}}(V,\rho)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{g^{\prime}_{1}}\eta_{(g_{1},h)}(V,\rho)}
\textstyle{F_{g^{\prime}_{1}}\circ F_{g_{2}}(V,\rho)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{(g_{1}^{\prime},h^{\prime})}(V,\rho\circ\alpha(g_{2}))}
\textstyle{F_{g_{2}^{\prime}}\circ F_{g_{2}}(V,\rho)}

(2.45)

and

\textstyle{F_{g^{\prime}_{1}}\circ F_{g_{1}}(V,\rho)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta_{(g^{\prime}_{1},h^{\prime})}(V,\rho\circ\alpha(g_{1}))}
\textstyle{F_{g^{\prime}_{2}}\circ F_{g_{1}}(V,\rho)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{g_{2}^{\prime}}\eta_{(g_{1},h)}(V,\rho)}
\textstyle{F_{g_{2}^{\prime}}\circ F_{g_{2}}(V,\rho)}

(2.46)

are the same.
The map in (
2.45
) sends
v
to
\rho(\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}))\cdot v
first and then to

\rho\circ\alpha(g_{2})(\alpha(g^{\prime}_{1})(h^{\prime-1}))\rho(\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}))\cdot v,

and

\displaystyle\rho\circ\alpha(g_{2})(\alpha(g^{\prime}_{1})(h^{\prime-1}))\rho(\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}))\cdot v

(2.47)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho(\alpha(\partial(h)g_{1})\alpha(g^{\prime}_{1})(h^{\prime-1})\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}))(v)

(2.48)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho(\alpha(g_{1})\alpha(\partial(h))\alpha(g^{\prime}_{1})(h^{\prime-1})\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}))(v)

(2.49)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\rho(\alpha(g_{1})h^{-1}\alpha(g^{\prime}_{1})(h^{\prime-1})h\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}))(v)=\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}\alpha(g^{\prime}_{1})(h^{\prime-1})hh^{-1})

(2.50)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}\alpha(g^{\prime}_{1})(h^{\prime-1})).

(2.51)

The map in (
2.46
) sends
v
to
\rho\circ\alpha(g_{1})(\alpha(g^{\prime}_{1})(h^{\prime-1}))(v)
first and then to

\rho(\alpha(g_{1})(h^{-1}))\rho\circ\alpha(g_{1})(\alpha(g^{\prime}_{1})(h^{\prime-1}))(v)=(\rho\circ\alpha(g_{1}))(h^{-1}\alpha(g_{1}^{\prime})(h^{\prime-1}))(v)

which is equal to (
2.51
).

Thus, the horizontal composition of natural transformations is well-defined, and the composition is
\eta_{(g_{1}^{\prime}g_{1},h^{\prime}\mathbin{\cdot_{\textup{h}}}h)}(V,\rho)
.

3
Loop group and string 2-group

3.1
Models of string Lie 2-group

We wish to construct 2-representations of String 2-group
\textup{String}(G)
from representations of loop group
LG
. To do this, we need a model of
\textup{String}(G)
involves
LG
.

There are two Lie groupoids in
[
MRW17
]
, both are models for
\textup{String}(G)
,

\textstyle{\widehat{\Omega_{\flat}G}\ltimes Q_{\flat,*}G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{\widehat{LG}\ltimes QG\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi}
\textstyle{Q_{\flat,*}G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{QG.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\xi}

(3.52)

Following the notation therein, we call the Lie groupoid on the left
S(Q_{\flat,*}G)
, and the one on the right
S(QG)
.
Here,

\begin{split}QG:=&\{\gamma\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},G)|\gamma(t+1)\gamma(t)^{-1}=\gamma(1)\gamma(0)^{-1}\;\text{is constant w.r.t. to $t$}\}\\
Q_{\flat,*}G:=&\{\gamma\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},G)|\gamma(0)=e,\gamma(t+1)\gamma(t)^{-1}=\gamma(1)\;\text{is constant w.r.t. to $t$},\\
&\gamma^{(n)}(1)=\gamma^{(n)}(0)=0,\forall n\geq 1\}\subset QG\\
LG:=&\{\gamma\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R},G)|\gamma(t+1)\gamma(t)^{-1}=e\}\subset QG\\
\Omega_{\flat}G:=&LG\cap Q_{\flat,*}G\end{split}

(3.53)

The letter
L
stands for loops and
Q
stands for quasi-periodic paths.

Then
\widehat{LG}
is the standard
S^{1}
central extension of
LG
and

\widehat{\Omega_{\flat}G}=\widehat{LG}|_{Q_{\flat,*}G}

is the restriction of this
central extension. They fit in the following diagram

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{LG}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{LG\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widehat{\Omega_{\flat}G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Omega_{\flat}G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}

(3.54)

There is a natural group structure on
Q_{\flat,*}G
defined by

\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}:=(\gamma_{1}|_{[0,1]}\gamma_{2}|_{[0,1]})^{\sim},

where
\sim
denotes to extend a path in
[0,1]
into a quasi-periodic path
2
2

2

One may verify that if we don’t define it in
Q_{\flat,*}G
but in

QG
, it will not be a good multiplication.
.
\Omega_{\flat}G
inherits this group structure and becomes its Lie sub-group. Moreover, we have a crossed module of Lie groups
\widehat{\Omega_{\flat}G}\xrightarrow{\partial}Q_{\flat,*}G
with

\partial:\widehat{\Omega_{\flat}G}\xrightarrow{\pi}\Omega_{\flat}G\hookrightarrow Q_{\flat,*}G

being the composition. This gives arise to a natural strict Lie 2-group structure on
S(Q_{\flat,*}G)
in (
3.52
), with
\mathsf{s}(\hat{\eta},\gamma)=\gamma
, and
\mathsf{t}(\hat{\eta},\gamma)=\eta\gamma
, where
\eta=\pi(\hat{\eta})
. Here the
product between
\eta
and
\gamma
is simply the one in
Q_{\flat}G
. See
[
MRW17
, Remark 3.4]
.

In (
3.52
),
\iota
is natural inclusion on both level, and by
[
MRW17
, Theorem 3.6]
, it is a weak equivalence of Lie groupoids if
G
is simple, compact and 1-connected. We use the symbol
\xi
to denote the quasi-inverse of
\iota
, which is the functor
\rho
in
[
MRW17
]
. They help to transfer the Lie 2-group structure to
S(QG)
, but this Lie 2-group is not strict any more. We recall below the explicit construction of the Lie 2-group structure on
S(QG)
in
[
MRW17
]
.

First we use a relative standard trick to reparameterise with a smooth staircase cut-off function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
.
\varphi
is like a stair-walk, with

\varphi(t+k)=\varphi(t)+k,\quad\varphi^{(n)}(k)=0,\quad\varphi^{\prime}(s)>0,

\forall n\geq 1,k\in\mathbb{Z},s\notin\mathbb{Z},t\in\mathbb{R}
. For example, we can take

\varphi(t)=\tau(t-[t]),

where
\tau
is the standard cut-off function on
[0,1]
and
[t]
is the integer part of
t
such that
t-[t]\in[0,1)
.

Then the formula of
\xi
on the level of
QG
is relatively easy:

\xi(\gamma)=\gamma(0)^{-1}\gamma^{\varphi},

(3.55)

where a path
g^{\varphi}(t)=g(\varphi(t))
. Then clearly
\xi(\gamma)\in Q_{\flat,*}G
, and
\xi:LG\to\Omega_{\flat}G
. To see how

\xi
lifts to
\xi:\widehat{LG}\to\widehat{\Omega_{\flat}G}
, and being defined on the level of arrows, we need to use the technique for classical
quasi-inverses in category theory. The formula is also longer.

To lift
\xi
to the level of arrows in a groupoid, let us first observe a simple fact: suppose that we have an embedding of a full Lie subgroupoid,

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 9.1743pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.1743pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{G_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 16.69455pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.1743pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1.5}\lx@xy@tip{-1.5}}}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\kern 33.1743pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{G^{c}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 42.3486pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1.5}\lx@xy@tip{-1.5}}}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\hbox{\kern-1.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}}}{\hbox{\kern-9.1743pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{G_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 16.69455pt\raise-26.82637pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\iota}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.1743pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 33.1743pt\raise-31.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{G^{c}_{0}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

This means that
G_{1}=G^{c}_{1}\times_{\mathsf{s}\times\mathsf{t},G^{c}_{0}\times G^{c}_{0},\iota\times\iota}G_{0}\times G_{0}
, and
\iota
are embeddings on both levels. The Lie groupoid morphism
\iota
being essentially surjective, which
means set-theoretically, that every element
g^{c}\in G^{c}
is isomorphic to an element
g\in G
, is expressed in the world of Lie groupoids, as the
following map being an surjective submersion,

G^{c}_{1}\times_{\mathsf{s},G^{c}_{0},\iota}G_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{t}}G^{c}_{0}.

(3.56)

Then to construct a quasi-inverse, we can adapt the idea from (discrete case) category theory
[
Mac71
, IV.4.1]
, namely we first find a map
G^{c}_{0}\xrightarrow{\xi}G_{0}
, such that
\xi(g^{c})\cong g^{c}
, and then given an arrow
g^{c}_{1}:\hat{g}^{c}_{0}\to g^{c}_{0}
,
\xi(g_{1}^{c})
is defined as the
composed arrow
\xi(\hat{g}^{c}_{0})\to\hat{g}^{c}_{0}\xrightarrow{g^{c}_{1}}g^{c}_{0}\to\xi(g^{c}_{0})
. This idea applied to the case of Lie groupoid, we need to find
a section of (
3.56
)

\sigma:G^{c}_{0}\to G^{c}_{1}\times_{\mathsf{s},G^{c}_{0},\iota}G_{0}.

(3.57)

Then
\xi:G^{c}_{0}\to G_{0}
will be

pr_{G_{0}}\circ\sigma,

which is exactly as in (
3.55
), and
\xi:G^{c}_{1}\to G_{1}
will be

\xi(g_{1}^{c})=(\pr_{G_{1}^{c}}\circ\sigma(\mathsf{t}(g_{1}^{c})))^{-1}\cdot g^{c}_{1}\cdot\pr_{G_{1}^{c}}\circ\sigma(\mathsf{s}(g_{1}^{c})).

(3.58)

In our case,

\displaystyle G^{c}_{0}=QG,

\displaystyle G^{c}_{1}=\widehat{LG}\ltimes QG,

\displaystyle G_{0}=Q_{\flat,*}G,

\displaystyle G_{1}=\widehat{\Omega_{\flat}G}\ltimes Q_{\flat,*}G.

Then we do have a
section as in (
3.57
),

\sigma:QG\to\widehat{LG}\times_{QG}Q_{\flat,*}G,\quad\gamma\mapsto(\pi_{2}\circ\sigma^{\prime}(\gamma^{F_{s}}\gamma^{-1}),\gamma(0)^{-1}\gamma^{\varphi}),

(3.59)

where
\sigma^{\prime}:P_{*}LG\to P_{*}LG\times_{LG}\widehat{LG}
is a section on “uniformly contractible loops” (or the space of paths of loops starting at
constant loops)
P_{*}LG
, and
F:[0,1]\times[0,1]\to[0,1]
is

F_{s}(t)=(1-s)t+s\varphi(t).

Notice that
\widehat{LG}\to LG
is a non-trivial

S^{1}
-principal bundle in general, however, since the space
P_{*}LG
is contractible, we may always find a section.

\textstyle{P_{*}LG\times_{LG}\widehat{LG}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}
\textstyle{\widehat{LG}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{QG\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{*}LG\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{ev_{1}}
\textstyle{LG.}

(3.60)

We give the construction of one such section below.

P_{*}LG
is the space of paths of loops starting at a certain constant loop. We fix this constant loop to be
\underline{e}
.
So
P_{*}LG
is contractible. Thus,

P_{*}LG\times_{LG}\widehat{LG}\cong P_{*}LG\times S^{1}

and we will denote an element in
P_{*}LG\times_{LG}\widehat{LG}

by a pair in
P_{*}LG\times S^{1}
.
We can define the section
\sigma^{\prime}
to be

\sigma^{\prime}(p):=(p,1).

(3.61)

Recall the element
C(l)
in
U(1)
associated to each piecewise smooth loop
l
in
LG
in
[
PS86
, Section 4.4]
defined by

C(l):=\exp(i\int_{\sigma}\omega)

(3.62)

where
\omega
is the integral closed 2-form defined
[
PS86
, (4.2.7), Section 4.4]

and
\sigma
is the surface in
LG
bounded by
l
.
Note that
\omega
is the Kac-Moody cocycle defined in
[
MRW17
, Remark 2.1]
.

The multiplication in
P_{*}LG\times S^{1}
is given by the formula in
[
PS86
, Section 4.4]
.

(p_{1},u_{1})\cdot(p_{2},u_{2})=(p_{1}\ast(\gamma_{1}\cdot p_{2}),u_{1}u_{2})

(3.63)

where each
p_{i}
is in
LG
from
\underline{e}
to
\gamma_{i}
,
i=1,2
.

In (
3.60
),
\pi_{2}
is the projection

\displaystyle\pi_{2}:P_{*}LG\times_{LG}\widehat{LG}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\widehat{LG}

(3.64)

\displaystyle(p,u^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mapsto u^{\prime}.

(3.65)

In other words,
\pi_{2}(p,u^{\prime})
is a lift of
p(1)
to
\widehat{LG}
.

Therefore, the Lie 2-group structure on
S(QG)
is the following: on both levels of objects and morphisms, multiplication and inverse are defined by mapping to
S(Q_{\flat,*}G)
via
\xi
and do the multiplication and inverse there. The horizontal unit is the constant path
1_{e}
at the identity
e\in G
. We recall the construction below.

Let
\epsilon:\xi\iota\Rightarrow\id
denote the counit and
\eta:\id\Rightarrow\iota\xi
denote the unit. Define the monoidal unit

I:=\iota(1)

where
1
is the monoidal unit in

S(Q_{\flat,*}G)
. The multiplication in
S(QG)
is defined by

f\otimes g=\iota(\xi(f)\xi(g)),\quad f,g\in QG.

(3.66)

The associator
a
of the 2-group
S(QG)
is defined as the composite

\displaystyle a_{f,g,h}:(f\otimes g)\otimes h

\displaystyle=\iota[\xi(\iota[\xi(f)\xi(g)])\xi(h)]\xrightarrow{\iota(\epsilon_{\xi(f)\xi(g)}\xi(h))}\iota(\xi(f)\xi(g)\xi(h))

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\iota(\xi(f)\epsilon^{-1}_{\xi(g)\xi(h)})}\iota(\xi(f)\xi(\iota(\xi(g)\xi(h))))=f\otimes(g\otimes h).

The right unitor
r
is defined by

r_{f}:f\otimes I=\iota(\xi(f)\xi(\iota(1)))\xrightarrow{\iota(id_{\xi(f)}\epsilon_{1})}\iota(\xi(f)1)=\iota(\xi(f))\xrightarrow{\eta^{-1}_{f}}f.

(3.67)

The left unitor
l
is defined by

l_{g}:I\otimes g=\iota(\xi(\iota(1))\xi(g))\xrightarrow{\iota(\epsilon_{1}\id_{\xi(g)})}\iota(1\xi(g))=\iota(\xi(g))\xrightarrow{\eta^{-1}_{g}}g.

(3.68)

For more details, please see
[
MRW17
, Section 3]
.

With the explicit formulas given above, we give some properties of
\sigma^{\prime}
and
\xi
below.
Lemma
3.1
will be used in the proof of Lemma
6.6
.

Lemma 3.1
.

The section
\sigma^{\prime}
defined by (
3.61
) has the property:

\sigma^{\prime}(\alpha p\alpha^{-1})=\alpha\sigma^{\prime}(p)\alpha^{-1},

for any
\alpha\in G
and any
p\in P_{*}LG
.

Proof.

For any
\alpha\in G
, let
\underline{\alpha}
denote the constant loop at
\alpha
.
Let
\hat{\alpha}
denote a lift of
\underline{\alpha}
in
\widehat{LG}
.
Let
p_{\alpha}
denote a path in
P_{*}LG
from
\underline{e}
and
\underline{\alpha}
. We may take
p_{\alpha}

going through constant loops connecting
\underline{e}
and
\underline{\alpha}
.

For any path
p
in
P_{*}LG
, we use
\overline{p}
to denote the path
t\mapsto p(1-t)
of the opposite direction.
Define the path

p^{\prime}_{\alpha}:=\underline{\alpha}^{-1}\overline{p}_{\alpha}.

We have

(p_{\alpha},1)\cdot(p^{\prime}_{\alpha},1)=(p_{\alpha}\ast(\underline{\alpha}\cdot p^{\prime}_{\alpha}),1)=(p_{\alpha}\ast\overline{p}_{\alpha},1)

by (
3.63
), which is equivalent to
(\underline{e},1)
since the area
\sigma_{0}
bounded by the loop

l=p_{\alpha}\ast\overline{p}_{\alpha}

is zero. Thus, the integral defined in (
3.62
) is zero.

In addition, by definition (
3.65
), we have

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(p_{\alpha},1)

\displaystyle=\hat{\alpha}

\displaystyle\pi_{2}(p^{\prime}_{\alpha},1)

\displaystyle=\hat{\alpha}^{-1}

Now we are ready to compute

\displaystyle\alpha\cdot\pi_{2}(\sigma^{\prime}(p))\cdot\alpha^{-1}

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}((p_{\alpha},1)(p,1)(p^{\prime}_{\alpha},1))=\pi_{2}((p_{\alpha}\ast(\underline{\alpha}\cdot p),1)(p^{\prime}_{\alpha},1))

\displaystyle=\pi_{2}(p_{\alpha}\ast(\underline{\alpha}\cdot p)\ast(\underline{\alpha}p(1)\cdot p^{\prime}_{\alpha}),1)

Let
l_{\alpha}
denote the loop consisting of
\alpha\cdot p\cdot\alpha^{-1}
,

\underline{\alpha}p(1)\cdot\overline{p}^{\prime}_{\alpha}
,
\underline{\alpha}\cdot\overline{p}
and
\overline{p}_{\alpha}
.
Let
\sigma_{\alpha}
denote the surface in
LG
bounded by
l_{\alpha}
.

By
[
MRW17
, Remark 2.1]
the integral closed 2-form
\omega
is a continuous cycle on
Lg
where
g

here is the Lie algebra of
G
. And from this cocycle we can construct a central extension

U(1)\rightarrow\widehat{LG}\rightarrow LG.

Then by

[
PS86
, Theorem (4.4.1) (i)]
, the differential form
\omega
represents an integral cohomology class on
LG
, i.e. its integral over every 2-cycle in
LG
is an integer.

Since
\sigma_{\alpha}
is a 2-cycle in
LG
, Thus, the integral
C(l_{\alpha})
is an integer. So

(\alpha\cdot p\cdot\alpha^{-1})
and
(p_{\alpha}\ast(\underline{\alpha}\cdot p)\ast(\underline{\alpha}p(1)\cdot p^{\prime}_{\alpha}),1)

are equivalent.
Thus,

\sigma^{\prime}(\alpha p\alpha^{-1})=\alpha\sigma^{\prime}(p)\alpha^{-1}.

∎

Lemma 3.2
.

\sigma^{\prime}
is a group homomorphism.

Proof.

For any
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
in
P_{*}LG
, let
p_{i}(1)=\gamma_{i}
for
i=1,2
.
We have

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}(p_{1}p_{2})

\displaystyle=(p_{1}p_{2},1),

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}(p_{1})\sigma^{\prime}(p_{2})

\displaystyle=(p_{1}\ast(\gamma_{1}\cdot p_{2}),1).

Note that
p_{1}\ast(\gamma_{1}\cdot p_{2})
and
p_{1}p_{2}
are both paths in
P_{*}LG
from
\underline{e}

to
\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2}
.

Let
l
denote the loop bounded by
p_{1}p_{2}
,
\gamma_{1}\cdot\overline{p}_{2}
and
\overline{p}_{1}
. Let
\sigma
denote the surface
in
LG
bounded by
l
. Then by

[
PS86
, Theorem (4.4.1) (i) ]
, the integral
C(l)
is an integer. So
p_{1}\ast(\gamma_{1}\cdot p_{2})
and
p_{1}p_{2}
are equivalent.

Thus,
\sigma^{\prime}(p_{1})\sigma^{\prime}(p_{2})=\sigma^{\prime}(p_{1}p_{2}).

∎

In addition, we have the property of
\xi
below.

Lemma 3.3
.

\xi(\xi(g^{c}_{1})\xi(g^{c}_{2}))=\xi(\xi(g^{c}_{1}))\xi(\xi(g^{c}_{2})),

for any
g_{1}^{c},g_{2}^{c}\in G^{c}_{1}
with
\mathsf{t}(g_{2}^{c}))=\mathsf{s}(g_{1}^{c})
. And

\xi(\xi(\gamma_{1})\xi(\gamma_{2}))=\xi(\xi(\gamma_{1}))\xi(\xi(\gamma_{2})),

for any
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in G^{c}_{0}
.

Proof.

On
G_{1}^{c}
,

\displaystyle\xi(g^{c}_{1})\xi(g^{c}_{2})

\displaystyle=(\pr_{G_{1}^{c}}\circ\sigma(\mathsf{t}(g_{1}^{c})))^{-1}\cdot g^{c}_{1}\cdot\pr_{G_{1}^{c}}\circ\sigma(\mathsf{s}(g_{1}^{c}))\circ(\pr_{G_{1}^{c}}\circ\sigma(\mathsf{t}(g_{2}^{c})))^{-1}\cdot g^{c}_{2}\cdot\pr_{G_{1}^{c}}\circ\sigma(\mathsf{s}(g_{2}^{c}))

(3.69)

\displaystyle=(\pr_{G_{1}^{c}}\circ\sigma(\mathsf{t}(g_{1}^{c})))^{-1}\cdot g^{c}_{1}\cdot g^{c}_{2}\cdot\pr_{G_{1}^{c}}\circ\sigma(\mathsf{s}(g_{2}^{c}))\mbox{ since }\mathsf{t}(g_{2}^{c}))=\mathsf{s}(g_{1}^{c})

(3.70)

\displaystyle=\xi(g^{c}_{1}g^{c}_{2})\mbox{ since }\mathsf{t}(g_{1}^{c})=\mathsf{t}(g_{1}^{c}g_{2}^{c})\mbox{ and }\mathsf{s}(g_{2}^{c})=\mathsf{s}(g_{1}^{c}g_{2}^{c}).

(3.71)

\xi(\xi(g^{c}_{1}))\xi(\xi(g^{c}_{2}))=\xi(\xi(g_{1}^{c}g_{2}^{c}))
because of the property of functors.
Thus,
\xi(\xi(g^{c}_{1})\xi(g^{c}_{2}))=\xi(\xi(g^{c}_{1}))\xi(\xi(g^{c}_{2}))
.

On
G^{c}_{0}
,

\displaystyle\xi(\gamma_{1})\xi(\gamma_{2})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1}(0)^{-1}\gamma_{2}(0)^{-1}\gamma_{1}^{\varphi}\gamma_{2}^{\varphi};

(3.72)

\displaystyle\xi(\xi(\gamma_{1})\xi(\gamma_{2}))

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1}(0)^{-1}\gamma_{2}(0)^{-1}\gamma_{1}^{\varphi\circ\varphi}\gamma_{2}^{\varphi\circ\varphi};

(3.73)

And

\xi(\xi(\gamma_{1}))\xi(\xi(\gamma_{2}))=\xi(\gamma_{1}(0)^{-1}\gamma_{1}^{\varphi})\xi(\gamma_{2}(0)^{-1}\gamma_{2}^{\varphi})=\gamma_{1}(0)^{-1}\gamma_{1}^{\varphi\circ\varphi}\gamma_{2}(0)^{-1}\gamma_{2}^{\varphi\circ\varphi}.

Thus,

\xi(\xi(\gamma_{1})\xi(\gamma_{2}))=\xi(\xi(\gamma_{1}))\xi(\xi(\gamma_{2})).

Moreover,

\xi(\gamma_{1}\gamma_{2})=\gamma_{1}(0)^{-1}\gamma_{2}(0)^{-1}\gamma_{1}^{\varphi}\gamma_{2}^{\varphi}=\xi(\gamma_{1})\xi(\gamma_{2}).

∎

3.2
Positive energy representations of
LG
and 2-representaions of
\textup{String}(G)

Recall that a linear representation

\rho:\widehat{LG}\longrightarrow Aut(V)

is of positive energy if its restriction to
S^{1}\subset\widehat{LG}
,
\rho|_{S^{1}}
,
is positive, i.e.
e^{i\theta}\in S^{1}
acts by
\Exp(iA\theta)
where
A\in\End(V)
is a linear operator with positive spectrum.

We apply our lemmas to a model of String Lie 2-group
S(G)=\widehat{LG}\ltimes QG\Rightarrow QG
.

We have a functor from representation category
\textsf{Rep}(\widehat{LG})
of
\widehat{LG}
to 2-representation 2-category of
\textup{String}(G)
. Since
\textsf{Rep}^{+}(\widehat{LG})
, the category of representations of positive energy of
LG
, is a full subcategory of
\textsf{Rep}(\widehat{LG})
, this further gives a functor from
\textsf{Rep}^{+}(\widehat{LG})
to the 2-representation 2-category of
\textup{String}(G)
. we have the corollary of Theorem
2.10
below.

Corollary 3.4
.

We have a functor
i_{string}
from
\textsf{Rep}^{+}(\widehat{LG})
to 2
\textsf{Rep}(SQ(G))
.
Explicitly, the functor is defined as follows.

•

On the level of objects: for
(V,\zeta)\in\textsf{Rep}^{+}(\widehat{LG})
,
i_{string}(V,\zeta):=(f_{V},\eta_{V})
as in Cor.
2.9
.

•

On the level of morphisms:
for each morphism

(V_{0},\zeta_{0})\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\rightarrow}\limits^{\omega}}(V_{1},\zeta_{1})

in
\textsf{Rep}^{+}(\widehat{LG})
, we define a morphism

i_{string}(\omega)

i(V_{0},\zeta_{0})\xrightarrow{(T(\ast),T_{\omega})}i(V_{1},\zeta_{1})

in 2
\textsf{Rep}(SQ(G))
below.
For each
h\in H
, we have the commutative diagram

\textstyle{(V_{0},\zeta_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{0}(h)}
\textstyle{(V_{1},\zeta_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta_{1}(h)}
\textstyle{(V_{0},\zeta_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\textstyle{(V_{1},\zeta_{1}).}

For each
\omega
, we can define a functor

F_{\omega}:\{V_{0}\}/\zeta_{0}(H)\to\{V_{1}\}/\zeta_{1}(H)

by sending the object

V_{0}
to
V_{1}
and sending each morphism
\zeta_{0}(h)
to
\zeta_{1}(h)
.
Moreover, we can define a functor

F_{\omega*}:C_{V_{0}}\longrightarrow C_{V_{1}}

by sending a morphism

\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{\rho}\{V_{0}\}/\zeta_{0}(H)

to

\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{\rho}\{V_{0}\}/\zeta_{0}(H)\xrightarrow{F_{\omega}}\{V_{1}\}/\zeta_{1}(H).

Then we are ready to define the morphism
i(\omega)

i(V_{0},\zeta_{0})\xrightarrow{(T(\ast),T_{\omega})}i(V_{1},\zeta_{1})

in 2
\textsf{Rep}(SQ(G))
in the way below.

1.

\displaystyle T(\ast):=F_{\omega*}:C_{V_{0}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow C_{V_{1}}

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{\rho}\{V_{0}\}/\zeta_{0}(H)\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\mathcal{G}\xrightarrow{\rho}\{V_{0}\}/\zeta_{0}(H)\xrightarrow{F_{\omega}}\{V_{1}\}/\zeta_{1}(H)\big)

2.

The natural transformation
T_{\omega}

\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces G_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nearrow_{T_{\omega}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{V_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{V_{1}}}
\textstyle{\textsf{Auto}(C_{V_{0}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\ast)_{*}}
\textstyle{\textsf{Auto}(C_{V_{1}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T(\ast)^{*}}
\textstyle{\Fun(C_{V_{0}},C_{V_{1}})}

is defined to be identity.

4
2-Vector bundles and Associated 2-vector bundles

4.1
2-Vector bundles

Inspired by the construction of 2-principal bundles in
[
SXZ17
]
, we suggest the following model for 2-vector bundles.

The
(3,1)
-presheaf
2\textsf{VectBd}
of 2-vector bundles:
\mathsf{Mfd}^{op}\to 2\textsf{Gpd}
is constructed in the following way: first of all, on the level of object, the 2-groupoid
2\textsf{VectBd}(U)
for
U
a manifold, consists of the following data

•

2\textsf{VectBd}(U)_{0}=\{U\times\mathcal{V}:\mathcal{V}\;\text{is an object in $2\textsf{Vect}$.}\}
;

•

2\textsf{VectBd}(U)_{1}
: a 1-simplex

U\times\mathcal{V}\xleftarrow{\gamma_{01}}U\times\mathcal{V}

consists of a functor valued map
\gamma_{01}:U\to\textsf{Auto}(\mathcal{V})_{0}
3
3

3

Recall that
\textsf{Auto}(\mathcal{V})
is the category of autofunctors from
\mathcal{V}
to itself and natural transformations between them. Thus
\textsf{Auto}(\mathcal{V})_{0}
denotes the set of autofunctors, and
\textsf{Auto}(\mathcal{V})_{1}
denotes the set of natural transformations.
; In other words,
\gamma_{01}
is a gauge transformation.

•

2\textsf{VectBd}(U)_{2}
: a 2-simplex with edges
\gamma_{ij}
for
0\leq i<j\leq 2
, or equivalently (in the model of bicategory), a 2-morphism
between
\gamma_{01}\circ\gamma_{12}
and
\gamma_{02}
, is given by a natural transformation (isomorphism) valued map
\phi:U\to\textsf{Auto}(\mathcal{V})_{1}
.

Given a morphism
U\xrightarrow{\psi}V
, the associated functor
2\textsf{VectBd}(V)\to 2\textsf{VectBd}(U)
is given by pre-composition.

Remark 4.1
.

Note that for each hypercover
U\xrightarrow{\psi}V
, the pullback
2\textsf{VectBd}(V)\xrightarrow{\psi^{*}}2\textsf{VectBd}(U)
is fully faithful.

For any
1-
morphism
f
and
f^{\prime}
in
2\textsf{VectBd}(V)
, let
\alpha:f\Rightarrow f^{\prime}
denote a natural transformation between them, which consists of a natural transformation valued map
V\to\textsf{Auto}(\mathcal{V})_{1}
, i.e. for each
v\in V
, we have an element

\alpha_{v}:f(v)\Rightarrow f^{\prime}(v)

in
\textsf{Auto}(\mathcal{V})_{1}
. The pullbacks of the natural transformation
\alpha
is
\id_{\psi}\mathbin{\circ_{\textup{h}}}\alpha:\psi^{*}f\Rightarrow\psi^{*}f^{\prime}
.
Note that, by definition, a hypercover on the 0 level is surjective. Thus,

\psi^{*}
on the Hom categories is faithful, since if
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}:f\Rightarrow f^{\prime}
are two different 2-morphisms, so are
\id_{\psi}\mathbin{\circ_{\textup{h}}}\alpha
and
\id_{\psi}\mathbin{\circ_{\textup{h}}}\alpha^{\prime}
. In addition, each natural transformation
f\circ\psi\Rightarrow f^{\prime}\circ\psi
can be expressed in the form
\id_{\psi}\mathbin{\circ_{\textup{h}}}\alpha^{\prime}
. Thus,
\psi^{*}
is fully.

Thus, by Definition
5.10
, the
(3,1)
-presheaf
2\textsf{VectBd}
is a 2-prestack.

Now we do a plus construction as in
[
NS11
]
to obtain the (3,1)-sheaf
2\textsf{VectBd}^{+}
which describes the entire data of 2-vector bundles, their morphisms and 2-morphisms. To do this, first, let us write out
\holim 2\textsf{VectBd}(U(M)_{\bullet})
for a cover
\{U_{i}\}
of
M
, where
U(M)_{\bullet}
is the Čech nerve of
\{U_{i}\}
. An object in
\holim 2\textsf{VectBd}(U(M)_{\bullet})
consists of

•

on each
U_{i}
,
U_{i}\times\mathcal{V}
;

•

on each
U_{ij}
, a functor valued map
\gamma_{ij}:U_{ij}\to\textsf{Auto}(\mathcal{V})_{0}
;

•

on each
U_{ijk}
, a natural transformation (isomorphism) valued map
\phi_{ijk}:U_{ijk}\to\textsf{Auto}(\mathcal{V})_{1}
between
\gamma_{ij}\circ\gamma_{jk}\xleftarrow{\phi_{ijk}}\gamma_{ik}
;

•

on each
U_{ijkl}
, a pentagon condition for
\phi_{ijk}
’s

\textstyle{(\gamma_{ij}\circ\gamma_{jk})\circ\gamma_{kl}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\gamma_{ik}\circ\gamma_{kl}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{ijk}\circ\id}
\textstyle{\gamma_{il}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{ikl}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{ijl}}
\textstyle{\gamma_{ij}\circ(\gamma_{jk}\circ\gamma_{kl})}
\textstyle{\gamma_{ij}\circ\gamma_{jl}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces,}
\scriptstyle{\id\circ\phi_{jkl}}

(4.74)

A 1-morphism in
\holim 2\textsf{VectBd}(U(M)_{\bullet})
from
(U_{i}\times\tilde{\mathcal{V}},\tilde{\gamma}_{ij},\tilde{\phi}_{ijk})
to
(U_{i}\times\mathcal{V},\gamma_{ij},\phi_{ijk})
consists of

•

\gamma_{i}:U_{i}\to\Hom(\tilde{\mathcal{V}},\mathcal{V})
which is a 1-morphism between

U_{i}\times\mathcal{V}\xleftarrow{\gamma_{i}}U_{i}\times\tilde{\mathcal{V}},

•

an element
\phi_{ij}\in 2\textsf{VectBd}(\sqcup_{ij}U_{ij})_{2}
which provides a 2-morphism making the following diagram 2-commute

\textstyle{U_{j}\times\mathcal{V}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{ij}}
\textstyle{U_{j}\times\tilde{\mathcal{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\gamma}_{ij}}
\textstyle{U_{i}\times\mathcal{V}}
\textstyle{U_{i}\times\tilde{\mathcal{V}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{ij}}

•

a higher coherence condition between 2-morphisms, namely compositions of the 2-morphisms on the five faces commute
